# Students

One folder per student, holding **everything** about them (D2, locked 2026-07-16): transcripts, correspondence, homework, notes, schedules, profile. These are internal working records for Joshua, Claude, and Codex, not pages for students to open. Open the student's folder and their whole working record is there. This is one of the brain's three knowledge systems, beside [[../wiki/index|wiki]] and [[../corpus/index|corpus]].

## Current students

- [[adriana-varea/adriana-varea|Adriana Varea]] · [[amanda/amanda|Amanda]] · [[asia/asia|Asia]] · [[avneet-natt/avneet-natt|Avneet Natt]] · [[celia-cutler/celia-cutler|Celia Cutler]] · [[charles-moore/charles-moore|Charles Moore]] · [[dakota/dakota|Dakota]] · [[david-salgado/david-salgado|David Salgado]] · [[ethan/ethan|Ethan]] · [[favour/favour|Favour]] · [[fernande-charleus/fernande-charleus|Fernande Charleus]] · [[gianna/gianna|Gianna Jean]] · [[ivory-johnson/ivory-johnson|Ivory Johnson]] · [[nicole/nicole|Nicole]] · [[ore/ore|Ore]] · [[sandra/sandra|Sandra Kooh]] · [[sofia/sofia|Sofia]] · [[steven-morris/steven-morris|Steven Morris]] · [[venusa-laveaux/venusa-laveaux|Venusa Laveaux]] · [[vinay-kalva/vinay-kalva|Vinay Kalva]] · [[whit-wilson/whit-wilson|Whit Wilson]] · [[yahunatan-zetvy/yahunatan-zetvy|Yahunatan Zetvy]] · [[zeina-borji/zeina-borji|Zeina Borji]]

**Taught in the last month** (2026-08-08 to 2026-09-08, transcripts pulled 2026-09-08): Celia Cutler (6), Adriana Varea (5), Steven Morris (4), Sofia (2), Vinay Kalva (2), Yahunatan Zetvy (2), Asia (1), Charles Moore (1), Fernande Charleus (1), Whit Wilson (1).

## Former students

Live in [[non-students/CLAUDE|non-students/]] as of 2026-09-10. Records preserved, excluded from every sweep. Read that folder's `CLAUDE.md` before touching anything in it.

## The primary/mirror boundary (D4 — read before regenerating anything)

Every file in a student folder is one of two kinds, and the distinction decides what a rebuild may overwrite:

- **Primary — the brain is the only home.** Transcripts, teaching notes, judgments, prep briefs, study plans, anything Joshua or an agent authored about the student. **Never regenerate, never overwrite; retain forever.**
- **Mirror — a projection of a live backend.** Session/booking state (Cal.com + Google Calendar), homework/dossier/publish state (LSAT U Supabase), payment events (Stripe), correspondence indexes (Gmail). On any disagreement the backend wins, silently.

**Convention:** mirror files MUST be named `*.generated.md` (or carry `generated: true` frontmatter). A file without that marker is primary by definition and must be treated as precious. Pre-2026-07-16 files (`email-context.md`, `payment-links.md`, `transcripts.md`) predate the marker — treat as primary until individually re-derived and renamed. No Markdown file here is ever the source of operational truth — no package balances, no running totals (all services are paid upfront; Cal.com answers sessions-used).

## Standard files per student folder

This is the target layout, not a checklist to satisfy with empty files. Create a record when its source or a real teaching judgment supports it. Leave an absent record absent until there is verified content to put in it.

- `<student-slug>.md` — internal entry page and folder router (primary). Its `## Open this record` block links only to local files that exist.
- `email-context.md` — synthesized Gmail context.
- `email-correspondence.md` — Gmail message-id corpus for the student's confirmed address.
- `payment-links.md` — verified payment, billing, and scheduling links.
- `transcripts.md` — transcript and meeting-note inventory. Its `## Local session files` table links directly to every full local Markdown export and every session package, newest first. A Google Doc link is provenance, never the only way to read a session.
- `teaching-notes.md` — durable, evidence-backed learning judgments and teaching patterns (primary).
- `open-loops.md` — unfinished follow-ups and pending decisions (primary).
- `live-status.generated.md` — currently a refreshable LSAT U booking-pass mirror. Calendar, Gmail, and payment state remain in their live systems; this file does not yet summarize them.
- `transcript-downloads/` — preserved local exports of Gemini notes (source evidence; do not hand-edit). A raw export here is not itself a completed session package.
- `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD-session-name/` — new self-contained session packages.
- `assignment-log.md` — running log of assigned work and notification evidence so repeats are avoided (primary).
- `Practice_Test_Reports/` — the student's PT score reports as received (intake form, screenshots, exports). Raw evidence — read for diagnosis, never edit.
- `Student_Specific_Homework/` — work assigned to this student specifically. Distinct from the shared banks in `corpus/Curriculum/*/Homework/`, which are the source material it draws from.

## Conventions

- New student → new folder `students/<slug>/` with a `<slug>.md` profile page.
- A consultation followed by a subsequent student session establishes onboarding. Check the Calendar sequence and identity before creating the folder; record any uncertainty about attendance or contact aliases. Consultation-only records stay in `_other-sessions/`.
- When Joshua explicitly says a student relationship is over or to close a folder, move the preserved record to `students/non-students/<slug>/`, remove it from the current student index, and refresh the generated roster. Do not delete the record or silently infer closure from inactivity.
- New session → `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD-session-name/` containing `transcript.md`, `summary.md`, and `topic-map.md`.
- Keep the original full Gemini export as a local Markdown file in `transcript-downloads/` when available. Build the session package from verified session evidence, and link both the export and package from `transcripts.md`; older exports may predate the package convention.
- `transcript.md` preserves the raw source. `summary.md` records the auto-saved summary, action items, and appended corrections. `topic-map.md` is a timestamped link index to relevant LSAT concepts, student records, and source material.
- Session summaries are append-only. Later corrections go in a dated correction section and never erase the original summary.
- Generated operational state is clearly marked with `generated: true` and never overrides primary teaching records.
- Wikilink student pages into corpus concepts and LSAT U material as they become relevant.
- Tier A note: transcripts and correspondence are trackable (Tier-A amendment, 2026-07-16). True secrets (credentials, IDs, payment instruments) still never enter Git — `private/` only.

## Internal routing contract after each session

1. Match the Calendar event and Gemini Doc by guest email, date, and content. Preserve the complete source export in `transcript-downloads/`; never rely on a Drive link as the record.
2. Create or update the session's `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD-session-name/` package without replacing a primary transcript or summary. Record any source mismatch as a dated correction.
3. Add a newest-first row to `transcripts.md` under `## Local session files`: date, direct local full-export link, local package links, and source/status. Include raw-only historical exports and packages even when the other half is absent.
4. Update evidence-backed `teaching-notes.md` and `open-loops.md`. Refresh the student's `<slug>.md` `## Open this record` block so Joshua, Claude, and Codex can reach the latest work in two clicks. Link only to files that exist.
5. Check that all local links resolve, that the full Markdown export contains the transcript rather than only Gemini's summary, and that the session index has no unlinked export or package. Leave attendance and missing-source uncertainty explicit.
6. Run `python system/scripts/check-student-records.py` from the repo root before calling intake complete. It checks all student routers and every local Markdown export and session package link.

## Operating contract

For each new consultation or transcript, Jay 2 must report what was found, what was created or updated, what was inferred, what remains missing, what is recommended, and what Joshua should confirm or reject. During the learning period, no student-facing message, assignment, or platform action is implied by record preparation alone.

# Student record audit, 2026-09-25

This is a dated snapshot, not an operational source of truth. Calendar, Gmail, Cal.com, LSAT U, and payment systems remain authoritative for their live state. No missing file below should be filled with an empty placeholder or inferred status.

## What was corrected

- Ingested the four native Gemini Docs attached to completed Calendar bookings from 2026-09-21 through 2026-09-24: Celia, Amanda, Sofia, and Sandra. Their existing session packages were retained. Full source exports, teaching notes, inventories, and follow-ups were filed.
- Added Gianna and Sandra to `students/README.md`; clarified the intended layout, the current LSAT U-only scope of `live-status.generated.md`, and the distinction between raw exports and session packages.
- Removed the contradictory `generated: true` marker from the primary session-summary template and seven existing primary summaries.
- Added frontmatter to Amanda and Charles assignment logs and Amanda open loops. Created evidence-backed teaching notes and open loops where this week's transcripts warranted them.

## Folder coverage

The standard files named here are `email-context.md` (EC), `email-correspondence.md` (EM), `payment-links.md` (PL), `teaching-notes.md` (TN), `open-loops.md` (OL), `live-status.generated.md` (LS), and `assignment-log.md` (AL). All 22 folders have a profile and transcript inventory. “Missing” means no file, not that the underlying event or information is absent. The table reflects the structure after the corrections above.

Every one of the 35 existing session-package directories has `transcript.md`, `summary.md`, and `topic-map.md`. Content completeness still needs a source check; Steven's short transcript demonstrates why filenames alone are insufficient.

## Calendar reconciliation

For 2026-09-21 through 2026-09-24, 14 confirmed bookings had ended. Four had attached Gemini Docs and were ingested. Ten had no attached Doc and no matching native Google Doc created in the window: Asia, Sofia, Sandra on 2026-09-21; Charles and Ore on 2026-09-22; Yahunathan on 2026-09-23; Angie Pasquel (consultation), Ivory, Venusa, and Sandra on 2026-09-24. Confirmed booking status does not establish attendance. Four further sessions were scheduled for 2026-09-25 and 2026-09-26 at audit time.

For 2026-09-14 through 2026-09-20, 14 teaching/consultation bookings were confirmed. Nine had attached Gemini Docs and matching local session packages. Five had no attached Doc and no matching native Google Doc created that week: Celia on 2026-09-14; Ivory and Sandra on 2026-09-17; Ore and Ritaj Abbasi (consultation) on 2026-09-18. The two other confirmed Calendar blocks were personal or ambiguous (`Liturgy`, `Online`), so they were not classified as missing teaching notes. Twelve cancelled entries were excluded.

## Next structural improvements

1. Verify which no-Doc bookings actually took place. Search alternate source types only for attended meetings; record a true missing transcript separately from a meeting that did not occur.
2. Populate EC, EM, PL, and LS only from verified Gmail, payment, booking, and LSAT U sources. Keep mirror files marked as generated and primary judgments append-only. The large count of absent files makes this a data backfill, not a formatting edit.

"""Check the local navigation contract for current student folders.

Read-only. Run from the repository root with:
    python system/scripts/check-student-records.py
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import re
import sys
from pathlib import Path


ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[2]
STUDENTS = ROOT / "students"
WIKILINK = re.compile(r"\[\[([^\]|]+)(?:\|[^\]]*)?\]\]")
MARKDOWN_LINK = re.compile(r"(?<!!)\[[^\]]+\]\(([^)]+)\)")


def section(markdown: str, heading: str) -> str | None:
    match = re.search(rf"(?m)^## {re.escape(heading)}\s*$", markdown)
    if not match:
        return None
    end = re.search(r"(?m)^## ", markdown[match.end() :])
    return markdown[match.end() : match.end() + end.start()] if end else markdown[match.end() :]


def linked_files(markdown: str, folder: Path) -> tuple[set[Path], list[str]]:
    paths: set[Path] = set()
    broken: list[str] = []
    for target in WIKILINK.findall(markdown) + MARKDOWN_LINK.findall(markdown):
        target = target.split("#", 1)[0].strip()
        if not target or target.startswith(("http://", "https://")):
            continue
        path = (ROOT / target) if target.startswith("students/") else (folder / target)
        if not path.name.endswith(".md"):
            path = Path(f"{path}.md")
        path = path.resolve()
        paths.add(path)
        if not path.is_file():
            broken.append(target)
    return paths, broken


def main() -> int:
    errors: list[str] = []
    folders = [
        path
        for path in sorted(STUDENTS.iterdir())
        if path.is_dir() and not path.name.startswith("_") and path.name != "non-students"
    ]
    exports = packages = 0

    for folder in folders:
        profile = folder / f"{folder.name}.md"
        inventory = folder / "transcripts.md"
        if not profile.is_file() or not inventory.is_file():
            errors.append(f"{folder.name}: missing profile or transcripts.md")
            continue

        profile_section = section(profile.read_text(encoding="utf-8"), "Open this record")
        inventory_section = section(inventory.read_text(encoding="utf-8"), "Local session files")
        if profile_section is None:
            errors.append(f"{folder.name}: profile lacks 'Open this record'")
            continue
        if inventory_section is None:
            errors.append(f"{folder.name}: transcripts.md lacks 'Local session files'")
            continue

        profile_links, profile_broken = linked_files(profile_section, folder)
        inventory_links, inventory_broken = linked_files(inventory_section, folder)
        if inventory.resolve() not in profile_links:
            errors.append(f"{folder.name}: profile does not link transcripts.md")
        errors.extend(f"{folder.name}: broken profile link {link}" for link in profile_broken)
        errors.extend(f"{folder.name}: broken session link {link}" for link in inventory_broken)

        downloads = folder / "transcript-downloads"
        for raw in sorted(downloads.rglob("*.md")) if downloads.exists() else []:
            exports += 1
            if raw.resolve() not in inventory_links:

# non-students — former students

Every folder in here belonged to someone Joshua no longer teaches. They were moved
out of `students/` on 2026-09-10 after he confirmed each one by name.

## What this folder means

- **Not a student.** Do not assign work, mint booking links, draft check-in emails,
  or include anyone here in a "current students" list, roster, or sweep.
- **Records are preserved, not deleted.** Transcripts, teaching notes, correspondence
  indexes, and homework stay exactly as they were. Nothing in here is ever deleted
  without Joshua's explicit confirmation, same as the rest of the vault.
- **Read-only by default.** Only touch a folder here if Joshua asks about that person.

## Coming back

If a former student returns, move their folder back up to `students/<slug>/`, set
`status: active` and `lifecycle: active` in `<slug>.md`, and regenerate the roster
(`python3 system/scripts/build-roster.py`).

## Why this exists

Before 2026-09-10 former students sat beside current ones with only a frontmatter
flag telling them apart, and that flag was wrong at least three times (Steven,
Adriana, Amanda). A separate folder makes the distinction physical: anything that
globs `students/*/` now sees only current students.

## Moved 2026-09-10

# Must Be True and Sufficient Assumption: Which Way Does the Proof Run?

The two question types can use the same logical chain. What changes is the missing piece. In Must Be True, the statements are already given and must prove the answer. In Sufficient Assumption, the conclusion is already stated and the answer must supply enough support to prove it.

name: gws-shared

# gws — Shared Reference

## Installation

The `gws` binary must be on `$PATH`. See the project README for install options.

## Authentication

```bash
# Browser-based OAuth (interactive)
gws auth login

# Service Account
export GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS=/path/to/key.json
```

## Global Flags

| Flag | Description |
|------|-------------|
| `--format <FORMAT>` | Output format: `json` (default), `table`, `yaml`, `csv` |
| `--dry-run` | Validate locally without calling the API |
| `--sanitize <TEMPLATE>` | Screen responses through Model Armor |

## CLI Syntax

```bash
gws <service> <resource> [sub-resource] <method> [flags]
```

### Method Flags

| Flag | Description |
|------|-------------|
| `--params '{"key": "val"}'` | URL/query parameters |
| `--json '{"key": "val"}'` | Request body |
| `-o, --output <PATH>` | Save binary responses to file |
| `--upload <PATH>` | Upload file content (multipart) |
| `--page-all` | Auto-paginate (NDJSON output) |
| `--page-limit <N>` | Max pages when using --page-all (default: 10) |
| `--page-delay <MS>` | Delay between pages in ms (default: 100) |

## Security Rules

- **Never** output secrets (API keys, tokens) directly
- **Always** confirm with user before executing write/delete commands
- Prefer `--dry-run` for destructive operations
- Use `--sanitize` for PII/content safety screening
Returns events on the specified calendar.

Usage: gws events list [OPTIONS]

Options:
      --params <JSON>        JSON string for URL/Query parameters
      --sanitize <TEMPLATE>  Sanitize API responses through a Model Armor template. Requires cloud-platform scope. Format: projects/PROJECT/locations/LOCATION/templates/TEMPLATE. Also reads GWS_SANITIZE_TEMPLATE env var.
      --dry-run              Validate the request locally without sending it to the API
  -o, --output <PATH>        Output file path for binary responses
      --format <FORMAT>      Output format: json (default), table, yaml, csv

## assistant

Tool call: Bash
{"command": "cd \"C:/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/da633bd6-3e61-4ab4-a9e9-83532f0067fb/scratchpad\" && python - <<'EOF'\nimport json, os, re\nfrom pathlib import Path\nS = Path(r\"C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os\\students\")\nev=json.load(open('events.json',encoding='utf-8'))\ncorpus={}\nfor p in S.rglob(\"*.md\"):\n    try: corpus[p]=p.read_text(encoding='utf-8',errors='replace')\n    except: pass\nallfiles=[p.relative_to(S).as_posix() for p in S.rglob(\"*\") if p.is_file()]\nrows=[]\nfor e in ev:\n    for a in e.get('attachments',[]):\n        fid=a.get('fileId') or re.search(r'/d/([^/]+)',a.get('fileUrl','')).group(1)\n        date=e['start'].get('dateTime','')[:10]\n        hits=sorted({p.relative_to(S).parts[0] if p.relative_to(S).parts[0]!='non-students' else 'non-students/'+p.relative_to(S).parts[1] for p,t in corpus.items() if fid in t})\n        # date-named files\n        dated=sorted({f.split('/')[0] if not f.startswith('non-students') else '/'.join(f.split('/')[:2]) for f in allfiles if date in f.split('/')[-1] or f'/{date}-' in f})\n        rows.append((date,e.get('summary','')[:50],fid,hits,dated))\nfor r in rows: print(r[0],'|',r[1],'|',r[2][:12],'| id-in:',','.join(r[3]) or 'NONE','| dated-files-in:',','.join(r[4]) or 'NONE')\njson.dump(rows,open('attach_rows.json','w'))\nEOF", "description": "Match each attached Doc ID and date against student files"}

# Student name aliases

Maps the names that show up in transcript filenames, calendar invites, and email
to the canonical student folder slug in `students/`.

It exists because Gemini names its exports from the meeting title, which is
whatever was typed at booking time: `Sofia`, `sofia`, `Sofia Minassian`,
`LSAT Session between Joshua and Meghan Mullins` all mean one student. Without
this file every pass re-derives the same mapping by hand and gets it slightly
differently.

Read by `system/scripts/transcript-inventory.py`. Format is one bullet per
alias, `alias -> folder-slug`. Matching is case- and punctuation-insensitive,
so only add entries the automatic matcher cannot get on its own (exact slug,
first name, and unambiguous containment are already handled).

## Identity & Contact

- **Name:** Zeina Borji
- **Email:** `zeina.borji5@gmail.com`
- **Location:** Ottawa, Ontario, Canada (Barrhaven area)
- **Background:** Lebanese-Canadian
- **Undergraduate:** Carleton University (Ottawa), B.A. in Political Science (concentration in International Relations)
- **GPA:** 3.7 OLSAS cumulative GPA (higher under best-2 / best-3 Ontario calculations, e.g., Queen's / Toronto)
- **Experience:** Government work at Global Affairs Canada, extensive volunteer work
- **LinkedIn:** [Zeina Borji](https://www.linkedin.com/in/zeina-borji-b16a6a24b)

## Goals & Timeline

- **Target LSAT Score:** 156–160 (conservative realistic goal; diagnostic at 148, previous tests 143–145, blind/3-section PTs up to 155)
- **Target Test Date:** November 2026 (primary target; January 2027 backup)
- **Target Law Schools (Canada):**
  - University of Windsor Faculty of Law (waitlisted previous cycle)
  - University of Ottawa Faculty of Law
  - Queen's University Faculty of Law
  - Toronto Metropolitan University (Lincoln Alexander Law)
- **Availability:** Fully dedicated to LSAT prep from September to January (no school or work commitments; 3+ hours/day Mon-Fri & Sun)

## LSAT History & Study Profile

- **Attempts:** Currently preparing for her 4th attempt. Previous tests include one with Logic Games (scored 143) and two subsequent attempts (one cancel, one in 140s).
- **Current Materials:** *The Loophole* by Ellen Cassidy (reading concepts, conditional reasoning), LSAT Demon (free version). Cancelled 7Sage previously.
- **Key Challenges:**
  - Conditional reasoning and formal logic diagramming/translation

  - Stamina/focus drop on 4 sections (drops from ~155 on 3 sections down to 145 on 4 sections)

﻿✍️ Quick notes
Please rate the new Quick notes tab by taking a short survey.
Zeina session


Sep 18, 2026
zeina.borji5@gmail.com


LSAT reasoning session covering argument structures with practice questions and study strategies


LSAT argument structure and core components
* Arguments comprise a main conclusion supported by at least 1 premise.
* Conclusions must represent author opinions rather than verifiable empirical facts.
* Evaluating statements requires asking probing questions to identify underlying support.


Intermediate conclusions and premise indicators
* Intermediate conclusions function concurrently as supported claims and supporting premises.
* Premise indicator words reliably introduce supporting evidence into the argument.


Argument categorization framework
* Argument statements categorize into main conclusions, support, neutral background, or opposing viewpoints.

# Other sessions — reference corpus

Gemini session notes for people who do **not** have a student folder in `students/`.
Consultations that never converted, one-off sessions, group sessions, and prospects.

This is a **reference corpus, not a student ledger.** Nobody here has a profile,
open loops, assignment log, or live status. Do not treat a file here as evidence
of an active student relationship, and do not draft correspondence off it without
checking whether the person is current.

## What it is for

Raw teaching material, kept flat so it can be read and analyzed in bulk:

- analyzing how Joshua teaches, explains, and runs a session
- extracting LSAT content, worked explanations, and question breakdowns
- formulating session scripts and consultation scripts from what actually worked
- giving the second brain more grounded knowledge of the domain and of Joshua

## Layout

One file per session, flat:

Every Gemini transcript attached to a student Calendar event in the last two months is now in the right folder, except five that need your call. Zeina's transcripts were all there already, but her records were stale, so I updated them.

## What I checked
I pulled every event on your main calendar from 2026-07-28 to 2026-09-28: 158 events, with 67 Gemini Docs attached. I matched each Doc to a folder by the guest's email and the date. All 67 are full transcripts, not just Gemini's summary.

## What was missing, now filed
Nine exports went into current students' `transcript-downloads/` folders, each with a row in that student's `transcripts.md` and a link back to the Google Doc:

| Student | Date | Type |
|---|---|---|
| Celia | 2026-08-03 | Session |
| Adriana | 2026-08-04 and 2026-08-06 | Sessions |
| Sofia | 2026-08-07 | Session |
| Vinay | 2026-08-05 | Consultation |
| Asia | 2026-08-06 | Consultation |
| Fernande | 2026-08-07 | Consultation |
| Ivory | 2026-08-26 | Consultation |
| Amanda | 2026-08-26 | Consultation |

Three consultations with people who have no folder went into `_other-sessions/`: Reet Minhas (08-07), Kyra Brown (08-24) and Cameron Stribling (08-31).

The link checker passes: 109 exports and 39 session packages, up from 100 exports. I haven't built session packages or teaching notes for the new files; they are raw exports only.

## Zeina
She's a real student: consultation on 09-10, then paid sessions on 09-18 and 09-25. All three transcripts were already in her folder, with teaching notes and open loops covering both sessions. What was wrong was the paperwork around them:
- Her profile still said `onboarding`. It's now `active`, and I regenerated the roster to match.
- Her main page didn't link the 09-18 session or the 09-10 consultation. Both are linked now.
- The 09-25 row in her transcript index had no Google Doc link, and a note wrongly said the consultation was left out. Both are fixed.
- Four of her files had `updated:` dates stuck at 09-14 or 09-24. They're current now.

## Needs your call
1. **Priyanka Nambiar, 2026-08-07.** The event is titled "LSAT Session" and it's a real tutoring session. She has an LSAT U account but no student folder. Is she a student? If so I'll create her folder; if not, her transcript goes in `_other-sessions/`.
2. **Lexi, four sessions (08-01, 08-02, 08-04, 08-05).** Her folder is in `non-students/`, which is read-only unless you ask. Want them filed there?
3. **Venusa, 09-11 consultation.** She declined the recording, so that Doc holds no transcript. Her folder already marks it as withheld until you decide whether to keep it.

## Found along the way
- **The roster's "last taught" column is wrong.** It lists Adriana as last taught on 2026-06-02 and Yahunatan on 2026-07-15, but both have Calendar sessions in September. So its list of "active but not taught recently" students is unreliable. The bug is in `build-roster.py`, which I didn't touch.
- **The roster now shows Yahunatan's pass as used up (12 of 12).**
- **Some sessions may have Gemini notes that were never attached to the event.** Several August student sessions show no attachment (Asia, Vinay, Yahunatan, Steven). I only checked attached files. A Drive search for loose Gemini Docs from those dates is the next step if you want it.

The sweep is logged in `wiki/log.md`. Nothing is committed; the auto-backup will pick it up.

## Record basis

Calendar has consultations on 2026-09-07 and 2026-09-11 with `lav.venusa@gmail.com`, followed by a 2026-09-24 event titled “Venusa session,” booked via LSAT U with `laveaux.law@gmail.com`. Under Joshua's 2026-09-25 rule, a consultation followed by a session establishes student onboarding. Calendar booking status does not independently prove attendance at the 2026-09-24 session.

The 2026-09-11 Gemini source records an objection to transcription and Joshua's agreement to stop it. Do not copy, distill, or use that source for teaching judgments while retention is unresolved. The source uses “Venuza Scarlett”; the relationship of that name to Venusa Laveaux is unconfirmed.

I deleted the Venusa recording everywhere I could reach, but two copies remain (below). Then I looked for improvements; those are ranked after the deletion notes.

## Venusa's 09-11 recording is deleted
- **Drive:** I moved the original Gemini Doc and its Google Meet folder to Drive trash, along with the three backup copies (the Claude Code backup and two Codex worktree backups).
- **On your laptop:** I removed the vault copy and the copies in both Codex worktrees. The Codex worktrees now each show one uncommitted deletion.
- **Her records:** her folder and the `_other-sessions` README now say it was deleted on your instruction and shouldn't be recreated.

**Two copies still exist:**
- **Drive trash.** It empties itself after 30 days, or you can empty it sooner. I'm not allowed to permanently delete things myself.
- **The vault's git history,** including the private GitHub backup. Removing it from there means rewriting history and force-pushing. It's doable, but it's a big step, so I'll only do it if you say so.

## Ways to improve the student folders, most useful first

**1. Nobody can tell where a student stands at a glance.** Profiles are mostly identity details plus filler; Sofia's snapshot just says "Current LSAT U student." I'd put a short "Where they stand" block at the top of each profile: current focus, weakest question types, target score and test date, last session, next step. It would be rewritten after every session.

**2. Open loops get written but never closed.** There are 91 unchecked follow-ups across the folders: Sofia 12, Zeina 10, Charles 8, Yahunatan 7. Only a handful have ever been checked off, so the file has stopped working as a to-do list. I'd do a weekly close-out that checks each loop against LSAT U and Gmail, then marks it done or drops it.

**3. Several "current" students look inactive.** Dakota, David, Ethan and Favour have no transcript after early July. Nicole's latest is from 08-14, though she had a session on 09-26. Worth the same yes-or-no pass you did on 2026-09-10, so the current list is real.

**4. Some folders have no teaching notes.**
- Asia, Fernande, Vinay and Whit each have sessions but no `teaching-notes.md` file.
- Dakota, David, Ethan, Favour and Nicole's notes have no dated entries at all.

**5. Session indexes have piled up.** Amanda, Ivory, Zeina, Vinay and others have two or three overlapping tables in `transcripts.md`, one added by each past audit. That's why two of my rows landed in the wrong table earlier. I'd merge each into one table.

**6. Folders come in two different layouts.** The July folders use email and payment files and have no `sessions/`. The September ones use `sessions/` and `recaps/`. Frontmatter has 5 to 7 variants per file type. One template, applied everywhere, fixes this.

**7. The 13 draft recaps have never been sent.** They're all marked unposted, some dating back to 09-08. Either posting them is a step nobody does, or they aren't needed. It's your call.

**8. Make the checks automatic.**
- The August transcripts were missed because the weekly intake only looked back to 08-24. A weekly Calendar sweep, like the one I ran today, would catch every attached transcript.
- The record checker only tests links. It could also check frontmatter, duplicate tables, em dashes and stale dates.
- The roster's "last taught" column is wrong (it says Adriana was last taught on 06-02). The bug is in `build-roster.py`.

Plus the fix list from the first audit: the 31 session packages with no frontmatter, Gemini survey junk in 10 session summaries, undocumented `recaps/` and `7sage.md`, and em dashes.

My recommendation is to start with 1, 2 and 5 together. They make each folder useful when you open it before a session. Then 8, so the folders stay clean on their own. Want me to go ahead?

71	Core structure and definition of conditionals
72	* Joshua introduced conditionals as strict rules or laws rather than simple sentences.
73	* Conditionals consist of a sufficient condition trigger and a necessary condition outcome.
74	Contrapositive and logical equivalence
75	* The contrapositive serves as the only other logically equivalent deduction for any conditional statement.
76	* Finding the contrapositive requires flipping the term positions and negating both elements.
77	Conditional indicator words and 5-step solving process
78	* Words like if, every, and all indicate sufficient conditions while must and required indicate necessary conditions.

81	* Mastering conditional logic requires automatic muscle memory, similar to learning multiplication tables.
82	* Conditionals represent the most common concept tested on exam day.
83	Logical fallacies and reasoning errors
84	* Students frequently struggle with conditional logic because the core concepts are counterintuitive.
85	* Confusing necessary conditions with sufficient conditions leads to invalid reasoning fallacies.

100	Summary
101	Conditional logic session covering sufficient and necessary conditions via rules and examples.
102	Introduction to Conditional Logic
103	Discussion defined conditionals as rules establishing guaranteed outcomes via sufficient and necessary conditions.
104	Diagramming and Contrapositives Practice
105	Exercise applied a 5-step process to diagram sentences and find contrapositives.
106	Test Applications and Homework
107	Review highlighted common logical fallacies on exams and outlined homework assignments.
108	Next steps
109	* [Sandra Kooh] Practice Conditional Logic: Practice the 5-step process for solving conditional logic problems repetitively until the method becomes second nature.
110	* [Sandra Kooh] Practice Logic: Practice identifying sufficient and necessary conditions in conditional logic exercises until proficient. Complete the assigned homework to reinforce muscle memory.
111	* [Joshua Fisseha] Send Materials: Forward the homework and practice materials to the student. Ensure the exercises cover variations of conditional statements.
112	* [Sandra Kooh] Ask Questions: Send a message to the instructor regarding any questions encountered while working on the practice exercises.
113	Details
114	* Introduction to Conditionals and Their Purpose: Joshua Fisseha introduces conditionals as rules, laws, or commandments that establish a guaranteed outcome based on a specific trigger. Sandra Kooh initially conceptualizes conditionals as a chain or connection between sentences or events (00:00:12). Joshua Fisseha explains that learning conditionals helps individuals apply laws to facts and make logical inferences, noting that the trigger acts as a sufficient condition while the resulting outcome acts as a necessary condition (00:02:36).
115	* Limits of Necessary Conditions and Contrapositives: Joshua Fisseha clarifies that a sufficient trigger represents a guaranteed way to reach an outcome, but it does not necessarily mean it is the only way to achieve that outcome (00:05:27). Joshua Fisseha introduces the contrapositive as the sole logically equivalent statement that can be deduced, explaining that if the outcome does not occur, the trigger must not have occurred either (00:06:43).
116	* Applying Conditionals to Geographical Statements: Joshua Fisseha uses an example regarding California and the United States to illustrate sufficient and necessary conditions (00:10:22). Sandra Kooh struggles initially with incorporating outside assumptions about other locations, prompting Joshua Fisseha to advise adopting a literal approach where they only focus on what must be true rather than what could be true (00:14:28). Joshua Fisseha employs a train analogy to explain that a conditional arrow must be followed forward like a train track and cannot be reversed (00:16:40).
117	* Step-by-Step Process for Finding Contrapositives: Joshua Fisseha details a two-step method for finding a contrapositive, which involves first flipping the sides of the arrow and second negating both sides. Sandra Kooh confirms familiarity with negation, and Joshua Fisseha demonstrates how this process applies to abstract variables like A and B, including handling double negatives (00:18:32).
118	* Evaluating Scenarios and Identifying Unknowns: Joshua Fisseha and Sandra Kooh analyze what can be deduced when the right side, or necessary condition, occurs (00:22:32). Joshua Fisseha explains that knowing the right side happens yields no definitive information about the left side, emphasizing that information can only be deduced by moving forward from the left side of the arrow (00:24:46) (00:27:58). Using a sports example involving Steph Curry, Sandra Kooh practices determining what is known for sure (00:29:04).
119	* Identifying Conditional Indicator Words: Joshua Fisseha explains that conditional indicator words dictate which side of the arrow a concept belongs to. Words like "if", "every", "any", and "all" indicate sufficient conditions that belong on the left side, whereas words like "required" indicate necessary conditions that belong on the right side (00:33:31). Using the example of entering Rwanda and needing a passport, Joshua Fisseha establishes the initial steps for diagramming conditional sentences (00:35:09).
120	* Practicing the Five-Step Conditional Diagramming Process: Joshua Fisseha outlines a five-step process for analyzing conditionals: identifying the indicator word, labeling the two concepts, applying the left and right side rules, finding the contrapositive, and stating the relationship back in English (00:37:08) (00:40:14). Sandra Kooh applies this process to the Rwanda passport example, correctly concluding that knowing someone has a passport or cannot enter Rwanda does not allow working backward (00:41:41).
121	* Practicing Conditionals with All Statements and Negative Concepts: Sandra Kooh applies the five-step process to the statement "All apples are sweet" and a historical statement about Ethiopia and colonization (00:44:49) (00:49:05). Sandra Kooh successfully identifies conditional indicators, labels concepts, applies placement rules, and determines contrapositives (00:44:49) (00:50:54). Joshua Fisseha warns against common pitfalls, such as assuming the first word in a sentence automatically belongs on the left side (00:52:16).
122	* Advanced Practice with Negative Requirements and Abstract Concepts: Joshua Fisseha and Sandra Kooh work through final practice exercises involving negative requirements for becoming a doctor and abstract bird categories involving flamingos and penguins. Sandra Kooh correctly identifies indicator words like "required" and "every", manages negative components, and determines contrapositives to solidify their understanding of formal conditional logic (00:53:04).
123	* Conditional Logic Fundamentals and Practice Method: Joshua Fisseha explains that mastering conditional statements requires focusing on the left side as sufficient and the right side as necessary (00:59:28). Joshua Fisseha compares this practice to memorizing multiplication times tables, such as 2 multiplied by 2 equaling 4 or 4 multiplied by 4 equaling 16, to build automatic muscle memory. Sandra Kooh listens as Joshua Fisseha outlines the homework assignment, which involves practicing left and right side identification before moving on to subsequent variations in future sessions (01:00:12).
124	* Test Applications and Logical Fallacies in Conditional Statements: Joshua Fisseha notes that conditionals are counterintuitive and represent a major hurdle where students frequently struggle. Sandra Kooh asks how this concept applies to test questions, prompting Joshua Fisseha to explain that conditional logic is the most common concept tested on exam day (01:00:56). Using an example where entering Rwanda requires a passport, Joshua Fisseha demonstrates correct deductions, such as knowing Michael cannot enter Rwanda if they lack a passport, alongside logical fallacies, such as incorrectly assuming someone who did not enter Rwanda must not have had a passport because the author treated a necessary condition as sufficient. Joshua Fisseha warns Sandra Kooh that poor foundational skills will cause them to panic during the exam. Finally, Joshua Fisseha agrees to send the practice materials to Sandra Kooh and invites Sandra Kooh to send a message with any further questions (01:02:01).

133	Administrative frustrations transitioned into reading comprehension strategies and practice drills.
134	RC paragraph structure and authorial moves
135	* Joshua reviewed the 4 primary authorial moves in RC passages: comparing, explaining, evaluating, and recommending.
136	* Comparing involves juxtaposing 2 competing ideas, theories, or positions within the text.
137	* Explaining elaborates on prior concepts, clarifies processes, and provides contextual support.
138	* Evaluating judges ideas as flawed, useful, or misguided to reveal authorial attitude.
139	* Recommendations state what should happen and establish the main point when present.
140	RC question types and analytical strategies
141	* Answer choices in RC function as must-be-true statements requiring 100% textual support.
142	* Joshua advised against spending excessive time on arbitrary analogy questions to maintain pacing.
143	* Conditional answer choices carry high evidentiary burdens and require strict trigger relevance.
144	* Question stems categorize into defined, semi-defined, or undefined targets to guide prediction strategies.
145	* Verbatim restatements of defined terms in answer choices require careful scrutiny.
146	RC answer choice evaluation strategy
147	* Answer choices require substantive meaning matches rather than exact verbatim matches.
148	* Expecting verbatim matches during test question evaluations serves as a predictable trap.
149	RRC methodology and paragraph breakdown
150	* The RRC process involves reading each paragraph and completing a full breakdown independently.
151	Next steps
152	* [Celia Syrocki] Practice Underlining: Underline the exact source sentence for every answer choice selected during Reading Comprehension practice. This process confirms the reasoning behind each choice.
153	* [Joshua Fisseha] Provide Drills: Send reading comprehension drills for the week focusing on paragraph by paragraph breakdowns.
154	* [Celia Syrocki] Practice RC Drills: Perform paragraph by paragraph breakdowns on the assigned reading comprehension passages to improve comprehension.
155	* [Joshua Fisseha] Create Reading Tool: Finalize and distribute a new reading comprehension tool for tracking speed and retention by the end of the week.
156	* [Celia Syrocki] Use Reading Tool: Track reading speed and retention using the newly provided instrument once it becomes available.

180	Details
181	* Administrative System Friction and Transition to Reading Comprehension Review: Celia Syrocki described a frustrating administrative process that required claiming a false family medical emergency to reschedule a commitment impacted by work-related travel, criticizing the rigidity of the system. Joshua Fisseha agreed that the policy was preposterous, and Joshua Fisseha then directed the discussion to resume a mini boot camp review focusing on Reading Comprehension strategies (00:00:52).
182	* Four Core Moves in Reading Comprehension Passages: Joshua Fisseha introduced a framework detailing four moves that authors perform across sentences and paragraphs: comparing, explaining, evaluating, and recommending (00:00:52). Celia Syrocki correctly defined comparing as examining two distinct ideas or viewpoints and explaining as furthering an argument or clarifying a concept (00:03:39). Joshua Fisseha emphasized active reading by focusing on identifying the specific job each paragraph performs rather than attempting to memorize details (00:05:38).
183	* Evaluating and Recommending Moves as Main Points: Joshua Fisseha and Celia Syrocki defined evaluating as judging whether an idea or theory is good, flawed, or useful, and recommending as proposing what should happen (00:06:45). Joshua Fisseha explained that evaluation and recommendation moves generally indicate the main point of a passage, with recommendations taking precedence, whereas science passages focusing on a past problem rely on summarized explanations (00:07:55). Additionally, Joshua Fisseha noted that the side with more passage dedication represents the author's dominant position (00:09:54).
184	* Passage Paragraph Categorization and Main Idea Selection: Celia Syrocki and Joshua Fisseha examined a passage paragraph that starts by explaining a speculative bubble and compares the differing opinions of Charles Mackay and Peter Garber (00:14:18). Celia Syrocki identified that Charles Mackay views tulips as a speculative bubble while Peter Garber disagrees (00:15:23). Joshua Fisseha guided Celia Syrocki to select an answer choice centered broadly around Peter Garber's perspective, emphasizing that Reading Comprehension answers must be treated as "must be true" statements 100% provable by the text with softer language preferred (00:17:05).
185	* Analogical Reasoning Questions in Reading Comprehension: Joshua Fisseha and Celia Syrocki addressed an analogy question requiring them to find a situation analogous to Peter Garber's defense of high tulip bulb prices (00:19:17) (00:22:19). Joshua Fisseha advised that analogy questions can be arbitrary and recommended not spending excessive time on them if stuck (00:21:40). Examining Peter Garber's argument that high original bulb prices are rational because earnings from millions of descendant bulbs can offset the cost, Celia Syrocki successfully selected the correct analogy of a publisher paying a high price for a novel and selling affordable copies (00:22:19).
186	* Evaluating Inference Questions and Conditional Answer Choices: Celia Syrocki and Joshua Fisseha analyzed an inference question regarding which statement Peter Garber would agree with, with Celia Syrocki initially selecting option B (00:27:06). Joshua Fisseha explained why options A and C were incorrect due to overly strong conditional language, option D was too extreme regarding 17th-century investors, and option E failed due to lack of passage support (00:28:20) (00:31:44). Celia Syrocki and Joshua Fisseha verified that option B was supported by Peter Garber's statement regarding original bulbs generating reasonable returns even if descendant bulb prices drop dramatically (00:34:56). Joshua Fisseha noted that quantifier words like "many" or "at least one" establish a lower burden of evidence compared to universal claims (00:36:48).
187	* Analyzing Charles Mackay's Claims and Question Stem Categories: Joshua Fisseha introduced a classification system for question stems based on target specificity: defined, semi-defined, and undefined (00:38:22). Analyzing a semi-defined question about what Charles Mackay claimed, Celia Syrocki and Joshua Fisseha reviewed the second paragraph where Charles Mackay claims the 17th-century Dutch tulip market was a speculative bubble (00:41:58). Celia Syrocki selected option B, which stated that prices were at least for a time determined by speculation, avoiding the overly extreme universal claims found in option E (00:44:15) (00:46:12).
188	* Determining the Purpose of the Second Paragraph: Joshua Fisseha asked Celia Syrocki to identify the main purpose of the second paragraph in relation to the passage's overall structure and main point (00:47:05). Celia Syrocki determined that the paragraph explains Charles Mackay's point of view and provides background context on the tulip market (00:48:00). Evaluating the options, Celia Syrocki selected option C, which correctly states that the paragraph explains the basis on which Charles Mackay claims the market was a speculative bubble, rejecting evaluation-focused alternatives (00:48:55).
189	* Interpreting Contextual Phrases in Reading Comprehension: Celia Syrocki and Joshua Fisseha tackled a question about the phrase "standard pricing pattern" located in the final paragraph (00:50:22). Joshua Fisseha prompted Celia Syrocki to analyze the function of the phrase within the sentence and paragraph. Celia Syrocki explained that Peter Garber uses the concept to support the argument that the tulip market was not a speculative bubble by showing that high initial prices followed by lower prices for reproductions is a normal cycle for new flower varieties. Celia Syrocki selected option D, which stated that the pattern regularly occurs in certain types of cases, with Joshua Fisseha warning against verbatim answer choices that restate terms without contextual translation (00:52:29) (00:56:58).
190	* Reading Comprehension Answer Choice Strategy and Drill Assignment: Joshua Fisseha advises Celia Syrocki that expecting verbatim matches in test answer choices is a trap, emphasizing that questions require a meaning match instead. After praising Celia Syrocki for successfully navigating a tricky passage and correctly identifying option D, Joshua Fisseha assigns a new weekly drill focused on the Reading Comprehension version of the RRC drill. Joshua Fisseha instructs Celia Syrocki to read a single paragraph, look away, and complete a detailed breakdown for every paragraph, mirroring the approach used for Logical Reasoning. Joshua Fisseha explains that this detailed paragraph-by-paragraph method simplifies Reading Comprehension by ensuring exact comprehension, and Celia Syrocki agrees to complete the assigned practice (00:59:14).
191	* Upcoming Reading Speed and Retention Tool: Joshua Fisseha informs Celia Syrocki of a new resource they are developing to help improve reading speed and retention for Reading Comprehension. Joshua Fisseha plans to finalize this tool near the end of the week, which will enable Celia Syrocki to track their reading speed and monitor how they progress through passages. Joshua Fisseha commits to providing the necessary instructions once the tool is ready (01:00:00).

full transcript. |\\n\"\n    t = t[:m.end()] + row + t[m.end():]\n    t = re.sub(r\"(?m)^updated: .*$\", f\"updated: {D}\", t, count=1)\n    idx.write_text(t, encoding=\"utf-8\", newline=\"\")\n\n    # teaching notes: append chronologically\n    tnp = f / \"teaching-notes.md\"; t = olp.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\")\n    t = t.rstrip() + f\"\\n\\n## {D} session\\n\\n{s['loops']}\\n\"\n    t = re.sub(r\"(?m)^updated: .*$\", f\"updated: {D}\", t, count=1)\n    olp.write_text(t, encoding=\"utf-8\", newline=\"\")\n    print(\"done\", slug)\n"}

I've updated the student folders for every session since the last update (2026-09-25 through today). The folders are now current. The link checker passes: 112 exports and 42 session packages.

## What changed since the last update
Sofia, Sandra and Celia each had a session on 09-28 with a Gemini transcript. Each student now has:
- the full transcript saved locally,
- a session package (transcript, summary and topic map with timestamps),
- a row in their session index,
- a new teaching-notes entry,
- the session's follow-ups added to their open loops,
- a link to the new session on their profile page.

These three packages use corrected frontmatter. The old template labeled every session as a "person", which the earlier audit flagged.

**What each session showed:**
- **Sofia:** her practice score is 159 to 160, and her goal for the first attempt is the low 160s. Her misses on hard Flaw questions come from structure she doesn't spot:
  - arguments with more than one conclusion,
  - an author who does the very thing they criticize,
  - "each regulation" read as if it meant "the whole set".

  Stamina is now the limiting factor: she gets migraines doing RC after LR. The plan is PT 130 and PT 131 this week.
- **Sandra:** first full conditional-logic lesson. She had the right idea on every practice sentence once she had the five-step process. What's missing is doing it automatically.
- **Celia:** RC taught through the four moves an author makes (compare, explain, evaluate, recommend). She did well on a hard passage. The weak spot is expecting an answer to use the passage's exact wording.

**Promises of yours that I can't see were kept:**
- Sandra's homework materials.
- Celia's weekly RC drills, and the reading speed and retention tool by the end of this week.
- The Sofia plan gets reviewed at today's session.

**Sessions with no transcript:**
- Nicole's 09-26 session has no Gemini Doc, either on the Calendar event or anywhere in Drive.
- Yahunatan and Sofia today haven't happened yet.

## The one big thing: a pipeline that turns each session into a prep brief
Right now, after every session, a person or agent has to do by hand what I just did for these three: find the Doc, save it, build the package, write notes and open loops, link it all. That manual step is where the folders keep breaking down:
- **Transcripts get missed.** 18 had never been filed, some from early August. The weekly intake only looked back to 08-24.
- **Follow-ups pile up.** There are 91 open loops and almost none have been checked off, because nothing ever checks them against LSAT U or Gmail.
- **You still can't prep from a folder.** Profiles hold contact details, not where the student stands. To prep for Sofia you'd read 8 teaching-notes entries and 12 open loops.

**The fix is one daily pipeline:**
1. It sweeps Calendar for sessions that just ended.
2. It files each transcript and builds the package.
3. It closes follow-ups that LSAT U or Gmail show as done, like an assignment that was posted or an email that was sent.
4. It rewrites a short "Before the next session" block at the top of each profile: current level and goal, what's working, what isn't, what's due, and what you promised and haven't delivered yet.

You'd open any student's profile five minutes before a session and have everything you need. Keeping the folders consistent, the problem this all started from, becomes automatic.

I'd build it as a skill that runs on a daily schedule, using today's sweep as the template. It stays draft-only: it never messages a student. Want me to build it?

question, and apply the method to new work. It is teaching content, not an email or a

No, there's no skill called "transcript intake." The one that moves transcripts into the right folders is **`session-recap`**, at [.claude/skills/session-recap/SKILL.md](.claude/skills/session-recap/SKILL.md). The "weekly transcript intake" name you may remember is just the title of a report in `students/_audits/`, not a skill.

Here is what `session-recap` covers:
- It sweeps the Calendar for sessions and exports the attached Gemini Docs.
- It files each transcript to `students/<slug>/transcript-downloads/YYYY-MM-DD-<slug>-notes-by-gemini.md`.
- It sends consultations and people without a folder to `students/_other-sessions/`.
- Its main job is writing the recap lecture note.
- It adds your promises to `open-loops.md` and flags things worth carrying into `teaching-notes.md`.

The skill doesn't do the rest of what I did by hand today:
- build the session package (`transcript.md`, `summary.md`, `topic-map.md`),
- add the row to `transcripts.md`,
- update the profile's "Open this record" links,
- run the record checker, `check-student-records.py`.

That's why things drifted: the filing step lives inside a recap skill, and the bookkeeping around it isn't written down anywhere.

A second skill you'll see listed, `lsat-session-lesson`, writes the student-facing lesson. It relies on `session-recap` for the transcript itself.

I'd split the filing part out into its own skill, `transcript-intake`, and add the missing steps. `session-recap` would then go back to only writing recaps. Want me to do that?
